height="20"><title class="sr-only">Share on LinkedIn</title><path d="M13.48,56.77V18.47H0.75v38.3H13.48ZM7.12,13.24c4.44,0,7.2-2.94,7.2-6.62S11.56,0,7.2,0,0,2.86,0,6.62s2.76,6.61,7,6.61H7.12Zm14,43.53H33.86V35.38a8.74,8.74,0,0,1,.42-3.11,7,7,0,0,1,6.53-4.66c4.6,0,6.45,3.51,6.45,8.66v20.5H60v-22C60,23,53.72,17.57,45.34,17.57A12.69,12.69,0,0,0,33.78,24h0.09V18.47H21.14c0.17,3.59,0,38.3,0,38.3h0Z"></path></svg></a></li><li><a class="group bg-faded-slate hover:bg-cyber-blue flex h-10 w-10 items-center justify-center rounded-full p-2 transition" data-social="mail" data-subject="Read this ZeroFox Article" data-body="Read this ZeroFox article at this url, https://www.zerofox.com/intelligence/brief-raccoon-stealer-version-2-0/" href="/cdn-cgi/l/email-protection#c1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="24" height="24" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-mail" aria-hidden="true"><path d="m22 7-8.991 5.727a2 2 0 0 1-2.009 0L2 7"></path><rect x="2" y="4" width="20" height="16" rx="2"></rect></svg></a></li></ul></div></div><div class="prose-module__ndhY7W__prose"><p class="">ZeroFox Intelligence has observed the following information as of June 28, 2022, and has released the following.</p><h2 id="executive-summary" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">Executive Summary</h2><p class="">On June 4, 2022, ZeroFox Intelligence discovered a then-unknown information stealer being distributed by ProCrackerz, a website distributing fake software cracks and key generators (keygens). The earliest known instance of this information stealer observed by ZeroFox Intelligence was a sample uploaded to VirusTotal on April 19, 2022. Twitter user @James_inthe_box <a href="https://twitter.com/James_inthe_box/status/1534593736356663296" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">suggested</a> the name “Recordbreaker” for it based on the use of “record” as the User-Agent string in each sample. In May 2022, logs for sale with “Raccoon Stealer V2.0” branding were <a href="https://medium.com/s2wblog/raccoon-stealer-is-back-with-a-new-version-5f436e04b20d" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">discovered</a> that matched what ZeroFox Intelligence was observing with Recordbreaker. Due to this and multiple other private confirmations, ZeroFox Intelligence asserts with MEDIUM confidence that Raccoon Stealer has returned and that Recordbreaker is actually Raccoon Stealer version 2.0.</p><h2 id="details" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">Details</h2><p class="">Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 is capable of targeting Chromium and Mozilla-based browsers by looking for well-known file names in specific directories. For Chromium-based browsers, threat actors using Raccoon Stealer 2.0 have the ability to specify a list of Chrome extension IDs and associated files as well. In ZeroFox Intelligence’s observations, these consisted entirely of cryptocurrency extensions. Raccoon Stealer 2.0 attempts to collect credentials, cookies, autofill data, credit cards, and data associated with specified Chrome extensions. For Mozilla browsers such as Firefox, only credentials, cookies, and autofill data were targeted. Other applications like Telegram and specific cryptocurrency applications can be targeted as well. To ensure that all cryptocurrency wallets are collected, a separate function exists solely to collect “wallet.dat” files. For any applications or files without specific support, a generic “grbr_” function exists to allow actors to specify files by path and a name or pattern.</p><h2 id="technical-analysis" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">Technical Analysis</h2><p class="">ZeroFox Intelligence first discovered Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 disguised as a crack for Microsoft Office on the ProCrackerz website. Clicking on any of the download links redirected the viewer through various advertisements and click trackers until they were eventually shown a set of directions and a Discord CDN link hosting the fake crack. The download links on ProCrackerz change regularly as the Discord links are removed.</p><figure></figure><figure></figure><p class="">The compressed files are small in size but inflate to hundreds of megabytes when decompressed. This is due to the samples being padded with large amounts of repeating bytes.</p><p class="">Samples ZeroFox Intelligence observed distributed in this way were obfuscated or packed; the unique string “edinayarossiya” was visible and used to pivot to other samples uploaded to VirusTotal. This allowed ZeroFox Intelligence to download much smaller (~56KB) unprotected samples and greatly sped up our analysis. Translated, Edinaya Rossiya means “United Russia,” which is currently the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Russia" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">largest political party</a> in Russia. Later analysis of the string decryption routine determined this is an encryption key for the protected strings used by the stealer.</p><p class="">Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 begins by importing all of the Windows API calls it needs (and some it does not). Importing API calls at runtime is a common tactic used by malware to avoid adding them to the import table to be used as a signature.</p><figure></figure><p class="">Afterwards, all protected strings are base64 decoded and RC4 decrypted as shown in <strong>Figure 4</strong>.</p><figure></figure><p class="">The RC4 key “edinayarossiya” was consistent across most samples, though some also used “credit19” instead. ZeroFox Intelligence is currently unsure if this is specified by each actor deploying the stealer or if this is decided by the authors for each build.</p><p class="">Unlike the RC4 key used to decrypt strings, the RC4 key used to decrypt command and control (C2) servers is a fixed length and changes with every sample. Aside from this, C2 servers are protected in much the same way the other strings are. Up to five C2s can be configured per sample, with each C2 slot hardcoded to be 65 bytes long. Addresses that are shorter than 65 bytes after being encrypted and base64 encoded are padded with spaces.</p><figure></figure><p class="">The locale on the victim’s machine is checked against two locales that can be hard-coded in the binary. ZeroFox did observe a check for a “ru” locale, but the language check does not affect the execution in any way. A second locale was not configured in the samples we observed.</p><figure></figure><p class="">Raccoon Stealer 2.0 also ensures that only one instance is running at a time by checking and creating a mutex. ZeroFox Intelligence observed this to be “8724643052” with every sample obtained. If it cannot open a handle to the mutex, Raccoon Stealer 2.0 will exit with Error Code 2.</p><figure></figure><p class="">The victim’s security identifier (SID) is checked against the value “S-1-5-18” to determine if the process happens to be running as the SYSTEM or LOCAL SYSTEM user. If so, Raccoon Stealer 2.0 will enumerate the list of running processes on the infected machine.</p><figure></figure><p class="">The first real action Raccoon Stealer 2.0 takes is to get the machine GUID and username, which are then sent as an HTTP POST request to the C2. As seen in <strong>Figure 9</strong> below, the GUID and username are sent together in the URL parameter “machineId” separated by a pipe character. The “configId” parameter shown is the RC4 key used to decrypt C2 addresses.</p><figure></figure><p class="">If the C2 is still available, the server will respond with a simple, newline-separated configuration. If no C2 is available, Raccoon Stealer 2.0 simply exits.</p><figure></figure><p class="">There are currently nine options that can be processed from the settings shown in <strong>Figure 10</strong>. A sample configuration returned by one of the C2 servers can be found <a href="https://gist.github.com/FirehaK/bb4f0c17c77d284bd5ad54187bbd199a" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">here</a>.</p><div class="rich-text max-w-full overflow-x-auto"><figure class="wp-block-table is-style-stripes"><table><thead><tr><th scope="col">Option</th><th class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left" scope="col">Description</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>ews_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Targeted Chrome browser extensions</td></tr><tr><td>grbr_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Targeted files to steal</td></tr><tr><td>ldr_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">A command, DLL, or executable to run</td></tr><tr><td>libs_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">DLLs to download</td></tr><tr><td>scrnsht_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Screenshot file name</td></tr><tr><td>sstmnfo_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Send system information to the C2 with this file name and add this template text</td></tr><tr><td>tlgrm_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Telegram-specific files and folders to target</td></tr><tr><td>token</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">URL path to POST stolen data</td></tr><tr><td>wlts_</td><td class="has-text-align-left" data-align="left">Cryptocurrency wallets and associated files and folders to target</td></tr></tbody></table></figure></div><p class="">DLL files downloaded using the “libs_” option are saved to the AppData\LocalLow directory. Raccoon Stealer 2.0 attempts to add this directory to the PATH environment variable but does not verify if it was successful. In our observations, this actually failed, and the sample continued to run without issue.</p><p class="">The following system information is collected during a run:</p><ul class=" rich-text space-y-2"><li>User locale</li><li>System time zone</li><li>Operating system</li><li>System architecture (32-bit or 64-bit)</li><li>CPU core count</li><li>Installed RAM</li><li>Screen resolution</li><li>All display devices (GPUs)</li><li>Installed software and versions</li></ul><p class="">Once each of these functions has run, another POST request is made to /&lt;token&gt;.</p><figure></figure><p class="">Rather than look for specific browsers, Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 targets any Chromium or Mozilla-based browsers by the name of the directories in which each respective browser stores its data. For Chromium, this is “User Data” while Mozilla/Gecko uses “Profiles.”</p><figure></figure><p class="">In order for Raccoon Stealer 2.0 to be able to read the data threat actors are interested in, it must load the DLLs from the “libs_” options earlier.</p><figure></figure><p class="">Data targeted from Chromium-based browsers includes:</p><ul class=" rich-text space-y-2"><li>Credentials</li><li>Cookies</li><li>Autofill data</li><li>Credit cards</li><li>Extensions listed in the configuration retrieved from the C2</li></ul><p class="">Data targeted from Mozilla/Gecko-based browsers includes:</p><ul class=" rich-text space-y-2"><li>Credentials</li><li>Cookies</li><li>Autofill data</li></ul><p class="">Although the “ews_” option is not necessarily limited to only cryptocurrency-related browser extensions, ZeroFox Intelligence has only observed this to be the use case. <strong>Figure 14</strong> below shows two more functions dedicated to stealing cryptocurrency wallets. The first, “wlts_”, exfiltrates files based on the configuration option of the same name. Other cryptocurrency wallets may still be stolen by the next function, which looks for “wallet.dat” files.</p><figure></figure><p class="">The “grbr_” function uses directory paths, file names or patterns, and other options such as file size specified in the configuration to decide which files it should exfiltrate.</p><p class="">The “tlgrm_” function is similar to “grbr_” but has fewer options. It is meant to target&nbsp; Telegram data, though the same functionality could have been achieved with the generic file grabber.</p><p class="">Taking a screenshot is separated into two functions. The first, “scrnsht_” checks to see if the configuration wants it to take one, and the second function actually takes and sends the screenshot.</p><p class="">Finally, the “ldr_” function is capable of allowing Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 to act as a loader for secondary payloads or execute commands. Each “ldr_” option contains multiple parts. It could contain a command to execute or the URL of a file to download, and if a URL is given a directory is specified to which the file should be downloaded. The last part specifies which action should be taken (e.g., execute a command, run EXE or DLL).</p><figure></figure><h2 id="recommendations" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">Recommendations</h2><p class="">ZeroFox Intelligence highly discourages seeking out pirated software of any kind. As in this case, such downloads are often completely fake and will not install the software the victim wanted. In some cases, the download may contain the actual software—as well as a hidden malicious component to infect the victim.</p><p class="">ZeroFox Intelligence also highly recommends that organizations take reports of pirated software on corporate machines seriously. With each download, the risk of infection increases.&nbsp;</p><h2 id="yara" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">YARA</h2><p class="">ZeroFox Intelligence has created a public YARA rule that can be <a href="https://github.com/FirehaK/YARA/blob/master/RaccoonV2/RaccoonV2.yar" target="_blank" rel="noreferrer noopener">found on GitHub</a>.</p><h2 id="mitre-attamp-ck" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">MITRE ATT&amp;CK</h2><div class="rich-text max-w-full overflow-x-auto"><figure class="wp-block-table is-style-stripes"><table><thead><tr><th scope="col"><strong>Tactic</strong></th><th scope="col"><strong>Technique</strong></th><th scope="col"><strong>Comments</strong></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Reconnaissance</td><td>T1592.001Gather Victim Host Information: Hardware</td><td>The sstmnfo_ function collects information about the infected system’s CPU, installed RAM, and display devices.</td></tr><tr><td>Reconnaissance</td><td>T1592.002Gather Victim Host Information: Software</td><td>The sstmnfo_ function collects installed applications and their version numbers.</td></tr><tr><td>Reconnaissance</td><td>T1589.001<br>Gather Victim Identity Information: Credentials</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 retrieves stored credentials from targeted web browsers.</td></tr><tr><td>Execution</td><td>T1059<br>Command and Scripting Interpreter</td><td>The ldr_ function can be used to run commands.&nbsp;</td></tr><tr><td>Execution</td><td>T1559.001Inter-Process Communication: Component Object Model</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 makes use of COM objects in the grbr_ function.</td></tr><tr><td>Execution</td><td>T1204<br>User Execution</td><td>Samples discovered so far relied on victims seeking out pirated software.</td></tr><tr><td>Defense Evasion</td><td>T1027.002<br>Software Packing</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 can be found packed in the wild.</td></tr><tr><td>Defense Evasion</td><td>T1140<br>Deobfuscate/Decode Files or Information</td><td>Strings and hosts to reach out to are RC4 encrypted and base64 encoded.</td></tr><tr><td>Defense Evasion</td><td>Path Interception by PATH Environment Variable</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 attempts to add AppData\LocalLow to the PATH variable.&nbsp;</td></tr><tr><td>Defense Evasion</td><td>T1070.004<br>Indicator Removal on Host: File Deletion</td><td>Several files are copied into the AppData\LocalLow directory and subsequently deleted after use.</td></tr><tr><td>Credential Access</td><td>T1539Steal Web Session Cookie</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 steals cookies from targeted web browsers.</td></tr><tr><td>Discovery</td><td>T1057<br>Process Discovery</td><td>If the process is running as SYSTEM, it will enumerate running processes.</td></tr><tr><td>Discovery</td><td>T1012Query Registry</td><td>The registry is used to gather system info, such as the operating system and currently-installed software.</td></tr><tr><td>Discovery</td><td>T1082<br>System Information Discovery</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 gathers system information, such as the victim operating system, system architecture, user locale, installed applications, and more.</td></tr><tr><td>Discovery</td><td>T1614.001System Location Discovery: System Language Discovery</td><td>User locale is checked, but no specific action is taken.</td></tr><tr><td>Discovery</td><td>T1124System Time Discovery</td><td>The victim’s time zone is checked and compared to GMT/UTC.</td></tr><tr><td>Collection</td><td>T1005<br>Data from Local System</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 offers configurable file-stealing capabilities for actors to choose based on their interests.</td></tr><tr><td>Collection</td><td>T1113<br>Screen Capture</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 takes a screenshot near the end of its execution.</td></tr><tr><td>Command and Control</td><td>T1071.001Application Layer Protocol: Web Protocols</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 uses standard HTTP requests to exfiltrate data and download files.</td></tr><tr><td>Command and Control</td><td>T1105Ingress Tool Transfer</td><td>Raccoon Stealer 2.0 downloads a set of legitimate DLL files to read browser data.</td></tr><tr><td>Exfiltration</td><td>T1020Automated Exfiltration</td><td>Data exfiltration is customizable by the actor through specified directories and file name patterns.</td></tr><tr><td>Exfiltration</td><td>T1030Data Transfer Size Limits</td><td>Actors have the ability to only steal files within a configurable size limit.</td></tr><tr><td>Exfiltration</td><td>T1041Exfiltration Over C2 Channel</td><td>Data is exfiltrated over HTTP and in plain text.</td></tr></tbody></table></figure></div><h2 id="iocs" class="my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left">IOCs</h2><div class="rich-text max-w-full overflow-x-auto"><figure class="wp-block-table d6e0f498b509599d81d15c0945325070cdc8c687</td></tr><tr><td>SHA256</td><td>f20dcb9477e356e91e1b00abc351f749739f98ca395840ae3062d6cebc72f85b</td></tr><tr><td>SHA256</td><td>f9c4d451f8c9d4e546e67348c4cc2e8810aa5c39d4fabe1ee891408a0bc53043</td></tr><tr><td>SHA256</td><td>fb26544d45a1166e15e37853786f0b98ff876d1ce94c240a0f3bc2f9a8fb258f</td></tr><tr><td>SHA256</td><td>fba1005e8c248ec460e6c13cb38759bd70d9db4882f88f651b194ab1800e656c</td></tr></tbody></table></figure></div><h3 id="scope-note" class="my-5 text-lg md:text-3xl lg:text-4xl font-semibold leading-none md:leading-none lg:leading-none mb-6 text-left">SCOPE NOTE</h3><p class=""><em>ZeroFox Intelligence is derived from a variety of sources, including—but not limited to—curated open-source accesses, vetted social media, proprietary data sources, and direct access to threat actors and groups through covert communication channels. Information relied upon to complete any report cannot always be independently verified. As such, ZeroFox applies rigorous analytic standards and tradecraft in accordance with best practices and includes caveat language and source citations to clearly identify the veracity of our Intelligence reporting and substantiate our assessments and recommendations. All sources used in this particular Intelligence product were identified prior to 12:00 PM (EDT) on June 28, 2022; per cyber hygiene best practices, caution is advised when clicking on any third-party links.</em></p></div><div class="border-gray mt-12 flex items-center gap-4 border-t pt-12"><div class="rich-text"><h4 class="h6 mb-2!"><a href="/blog/author/zerofox-team/">ZeroFox Team</a></h4></div></div><p class="mt-4 mb-0"><strong>Tags: </strong><span><a href="/topic/cyber-trends/">Cyber Trends</a>, </span><span><a href="/topic/threat-intelligence/">Threat Stealer version 2.0 is capable of targeting Chromium and Mozilla-based browsers by looking for well-known file names in specific directories. For Chromium-based browsers, threat actors using Raccoon Stealer 2.0 have the ability to specify a list of Chrome extension IDs and associated files as well. In ZeroFox Intelligence’s observations, these consisted entirely of cryptocurrency extensions. Raccoon Stealer 2.0 attempts to collect credentials, cookies, autofill data, credit cards, and data associated with specified Chrome extensions. For Mozilla browsers such as Firefox, only credentials, cookies, and autofill data were targeted. Other applications like Telegram and specific cryptocurrency applications can be targeted as well. To ensure that all cryptocurrency wallets are collected, a separate function exists solely to collect “wallet.dat” files. For any applications or files without specific support, a generic “grbr_” function exists to allow actors to specify files by path and a name or pattern.\"}}]\n38:[\"$\",\"h2\",\"a5231011-c561-420b-a809-213161ccad3f\",{\"dangerouslySetInnerHTML\":{\"__html\":\"Technical Analysis\"},\"id\":\"technical-analysis\",\"className\":\"my-5 text-2xl md:text-5xl lg:text-6xl font-semibold mb-3 text-left\"}]\n39:[\"$\",\"p\",\"093df3b0-13f1-4666-b88c-53e27ac4df4a\",{\"className\":\"\",\"dangerouslySetInnerHTML\":{\"__html\":\"ZeroFox Intelligence first discovered Raccoon Stealer version 2.0 disguised as a crack for Microsoft Office on the ProCrackerz website.
